#7307. How CeraVe’s ‘Dune’ deal takes the brand’s social-first playbook global

The L’Oréal brand’s first film partnership makes it the “Official Moisturizer of Arrakis” ahead of the blockbuster’s Dec. 18 release.

CeraVe last week partnered with “Dune: Part Three” to become the “Official Moisturizer of Arrakis,” the desert planet that serves as the sci-fi epic’s titular setting. The tie-up with Warner Bros. Pictures and Legendary Pictures represents the L'Oréal brand’s first film partnership, with a campaign that will roll out ahead of the movie’s Dec. 18 theatrical release.

The brand on Monday revealed the next chapter in the campaign. A humorous “Sandworm Wrangler” video, shot in the style of behind-the-scenes content, shows how a wrangler of one of the film’s iconic sandworms uses CeraVe Moisturizing Cream — which now offers 120 hours of hydration — on the dry-skinned creature.

The debut “Precious Resource” spot reimagined the lore of Arrakis, an arid planet where every drop of moisture is preserved, replacing the story’s central resource, spice, with CeraVe. The “Dune” partnership is the latest iteration of a “medutainment” strategy that combines science with entertainment and has helped grow CeraVe into a $2 billion brand.

“When it comes to the playbook, that's something we are reproducing here with ‘Dune,’” said Vincent Chauvière, global brand president at CeraVe. “It's a multipoint campaign that is social-first, starting with a bit of excitement and surprise, and then we are going full media.”

Along with co-branded content, the campaign spans paid, earned, social, digital, retail, creator and experiential activations. The brand will also launch out-of-home ads, creator gifting and corporate screening experiences, as well as sponsor film premiere events in Los Angeles and the U.K.

“Our goal is to create partnerships that extend the worlds of our films in ways that feel unexpected, culturally relevant and genuinely entertaining,” Julie Moore, executive vice president, global brand partnerships at Warner Bros. Pictures, said in a statement.

The “Dune” campaign was created and produced by OBB Bolded, OBB’s branded content studio, with content filmed in Samalayuca Dunes in Mexico and Mexico City and directed by Maca Rubio from Stink Films. OBB was also behind an award-winning “Moisturize Like a Derm” effort that appointed NBA star Kevin Durant as the brand’s “Face of Legs.”

“It's been a campaign that has been working at bringing awareness to the brand, but also to the usage of a body moisturizer, bringing our science to new consumers, particularly men. We've been recruiting more men this year, thanks to this partnership [with the NBA],” Chauvière explained.

The “Dune” partnership is rolling out globally, and the campaign is designed to pair content around the billion-dollar franchise with market-specific dermatologists and experiential activations. CeraVe is growing at a double-digit rate this year and gaining share around the world, and geographic expansion is key to the brand’s future growth, Chauvière said.

“The partnership with ‘Dune’ [is] a way to have a more consistent brand tone of voice and point of view. We have local teams that have a lot of local activations, and the influencer world is a local world. So it's all all about the balance,” the executive said.

The local push builds on CeraVe’s larger creator strategy, which helps the brand reach more than 15 million followers across platforms. The brand this summer united over 100 creators from 22 countries for its first Global CerAwards in Los Angeles. The event included CeraVe Studios, an immersive content creation experience hosted on a Hollywood studio lot, that generated nearly 60 million views of new content, per Chauvière.

“It was a moment of visibility, but it's also a moment to gather all the local influencers together and reunite around this idea of bringing creativity in the beauty world and making science accessible and entertaining at the same time,” the executive said.

#7305. Graduate Engineer (remote work) at CloudLinux

Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

You don’t need prior experience in these specific areas. We’re looking for strong engineering potential — how you approach unfamiliar problems, learn quickly and use AI thoughtfully. We’ll consider your challenge results, interests and strengths alongside the opportunities available across our engineering teams.

#7304. What it will take for CMOs to reach the next frontier of AI-driven growth

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

CMOs at the largest enterprise brands across consumer goods, financial services, B2B and more are all struggling with the same thing: more AI technology has not translated into more business growth. They’re stuck chasing efficiency gains, funding tools that optimize tasks inside functional silos, running pilots that go nowhere and delegating AI decisions to IT teams.

But a few CMOs are doing it differently. Eric Schwartz, CMO of The Clorox Company, told me his marketers are building AI systems that produce highly optimized product content. They’re encoding brand voice, compliance guardrails and seasonal context into agents that work across every brand in their portfolio. Their goal isn’t to save time on doing what human writers can already do. Instead, they want to codify, scale and orchestrate the expertise that increases relevance, engagement and conversion with consumers. That’s a growth play.

This is an operating-model decision that the CMO has to own: give marketers the strategic, system-building work and shift execution to AI agents. That takes much more than a chatbot built for a solo work experience.

“I tell everyone that tries to pitch me about AI capabilities that the last thing I need is a 14th chatbot to save our marketers time,” Schwartz said. “What I need is interoperable functionality.”

Schwartz isn’t alone. A vanguard of marketers are charting the frontier of AI-native marketing. To do this requires three fundamental shifts.

Employees are becoming more efficient with AI, but report that leaders aren’t asking them to reinvest the productivity gains into strategic work; they’re just expected to produce more output. Boston Consulting Group found that 66% of frontline employees receive limited or no guidance on using saved time, and more than half don’t reinvest it into strategic work.

Measuring AI success solely on efficiency invites efficiency’s reward: a smaller budget.

For example, one CMO’s AI-powered content system is saving the company 500 hours a month. But the company’s CFO saw the dashboard and asked: “If marketing can do this with 500 fewer hours, why does the budget stay the same?” The dashboard was supposed to prove value. Instead, it made the case for a cut.

A marketing team at a regulated financial services company chose a different approach. The team was losing 360 hours a week to legal review bottlenecks, manual multi-channel content creation and constant product updates across 800-plus references. The team built a system with compliance encoded into the workflow and distribution automated across 25 channels. The result: 360 hours freed up weekly, 90% less editing time and zero agency spend.

The marketing team reinvested those hours into producing an award-winning ad campaign, not producing more of the same content. In a category where most consumers can’t name three financial lenders, breaking through the sameness drove brand recall, pipeline and market share. The system handled the production, but the humans did the work that won.

The AI opportunity isn’t time saved. Frontier CMOs reinvest that time in architecture, curation and orchestration: designing agentic workflows, encoding brand voice and steering the system to drive growth. The humans who know best what makes their brand different turn saved time into a real advantage.

Historically, marketing and revenue have operated on separate tracks. In B2B, marketing fills the top of the funnel and hands off to sales. In consumer brands, marketing creates content while commerce manages the shelf. In financial services, brand marketing and the advisors who own the relationship rarely connect. In most organizations, no single team owns the moments that decide the sale.

Sixty-seven percent of B2B buyers now prefer a rep-free experience, according to data from Gartner. Consumers and bank customers are doing their own research too. In financial services, buyers are asking AI tools for recommendations before they ever talk to an advisor. These buyers aren’t waiting for a handoff.At the frontier, there are no handoffs. AI agents carry customer data, market signals and brand and compliance context between marketing and revenue teams in real time. Context doesn’t die at a cross-functional boundary. It compounds.

Schwartz described what this looks like at Clorox. The company’s media ecosystem spans retail media, e-commerce and traditional retail, and the goal is to connect performance back to sales — or to the signals that point to sales. Most marketing organizations stop at creating and deploying campaigns. Schwartz wants that signal flowing back to show where to lean in and scale and to shape what gets created next. By closing that loop, a media budget compounds into growth instead of disappearing.

A CMO recently told me his organization has 40 active pilots, all marked green. Everything worked in the lab, but nothing survived contact with the organization. IT loved the tools, but marketing couldn’t use them.

This happens when AI adoption is delegated to IT. The CMO sponsors the budget but doesn’t own the transformation. In Writer’s 2026 survey, more than half of CMOs (52.3%) reported “significant knowledge gaps” about AI. Gartner predicts that by 2027, lack of AI literacy will rank among the top three reasons CMOs are replaced.

At the frontier, the CMO owns the entire marketing AI agenda: selecting tools, redesigning workflows and deciding what should remain human-led. That means making AI adoption a cultural shift, not a software rollout. And that can’t be delegated.

Schwartz is an example of what this looks like in practice. He made AI adoption his own rather than delegating it to an innovation team or task force.

The CMOs making progress are changing how the work gets done, moving from traditional marketing operations toward a new model of driving growth with AI. They aren’t simply doing the same work with AI layered on top, but reimagining foundational structures, systems and strategies for a new era of marketing. The frontier isn’t a place a CMO arrives at. It’s the way a CMO operates.Partner insights from Writer

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

#7301. Amazon puts Arnold Schwarzenegger in Santa suit to extend holiday push

A new spot ties into the upcoming movie “The Man with the Bag,” showing Amazon again tapping into its own programming to promote its range of offerings.

Amazon is once again tapping into its Prime ecosystem to integrate one of its entertainment properties into an advertising campaign. Taking inspiration from “The Man with the Bag,” which premiers Dec. 2, the e-commerce platform has created a light-hearted holiday ad featuring a familiar face.

“Santa Schwarzenegger” follows the actor as he uses a Santa suit, delivered by Prime, to explore New York City unnoticed. During his adventure, Schwarzenegger is able to watch a game with bar patrons, catch a holiday movie and use his strength to help a family get a Christmas tree on the roof of their car. It ends with the actor using an Amazon Locker to pick up a pair of skates so he can enjoy time on an ice rink. The minute-long spot is set to “Make Someone Happy” by Jimmy Durante.

Schwarzenegger will also appear as Santa throughout the season outside of the spot and movie, per press details.

Amazon has made finding moments of joy a central part of its holiday strategy. “Santa Schwarzenegger” follows the longer “Joy Delivered” spot, which tells the story of a young woman going on a “Planes, Trains and Automobiles”-style journey to make it home to her family in time for the holidays, giving away the gifts she bought along the way.

This is not the first time Amazon has drawn inspiration from a Prime Video title in its advertising. In May, Amazon’s Brand Innovation Lab released a spot with Liquid I.V. featuring the leading actors from its hockey romance show, “Off Campus,” marking the first time Prime Video leads appeared in a co-branded spot.

“Santa Schwarzenegger” allows the e-commerce giant to promote its delivery services while also showing how its properties can be used for advertising.

Amazon’s ad revenue hit $19.6 billion in Q3, a 12% year-over-year increase, per an earnings statement. However, this is a significant dip from the 26% YoY growth rate seen just a quarter earlier, leading to a stock dip for the company.

#7299. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer - Toronto Remote at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

ProcessOS is a new kind of Camunda product: business processes described in natural language, generated as executable BPMN workflows, and improved autonomously from production data. We’re hiring the first cohort of Forward Deployed Engineers to bring it into production at enterprise customers. You’ll own the deployment journey from first process file to a working, improving system, and hand off a functioning capability to the customer’s team. The code you write and the patterns you discover feed directly back into how ProcessOS is built. This is a rare opportunity to shape a product, a function, and a field practice from day one.

You’ll work end‑to‑end across Java services and React interfaces, collaborating closely with a talented global team to measure, improve, and scale performance and fault tolerance in real-world distributed systems.

Deploy and prove. Own the journey from first process file to production workflow. At 90 days: at least one workflow running, the improvement loop active, and measurable fitness gains the customer can see. At 12 months: 3–5 customers with viable deployments.

Drive organizational adoption. Work across technical and business stakeholders to build understanding and trust, turning early deployments into lasting customer capability.

Work at depth technically. Configure and run Camunda clusters locally and in cloud environments. Debug integration failures. Build custom agents where platform defaults don’t fit. Write connector logic that gets legacy enterprise systems talking to ProcessOS.

Build the playbook. There is no deployment template yet. Document what good looks like as you discover it, and make the next implementation faster.

Feed the product. Custom agents you write get contributed back. Integration patterns get codified. Production bugs you find, you often fix. Your field work is a direct input to ProcessOS.

Hand off deliberately. Leave customers with a team that can operate independently, is trained on the platform, and acts on improvement results without you.

Enterprise production experience: you’ve shipped something inside a large organization and stayed to see it operate. You know why governance and resilience matter at scale.

Technical depth: You can debug an integration failure, write a custom agent against a legacy API, stand up a Camunda cluster from scratch, and explain generated BPMN to both engineers and business leads.

Stakeholder communication: you work effectively across engineering, architecture, and business leadership and adapt how you communicate without losing precision.

Practical AI experience: you’ve used AI to solve real problems in production – not just experimented. You understand context management, prompt engineering, agent testing, and integration tradeoffs.

Comfort with ambiguity: you make sound judgment calls without a playbook and document what you learn.

Experience in a customer-facing engineering or professional services role.

#7298. Anatomy of an influencer marketing deal

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Ever wonder how an influencer marketing deal gets negotiated? Well, you’re in luck.

In a live Digiday Podcast recording during the recent Creator Strategies virtual event, YSL Beauty’s Hadley Musinsky and Night’s Sophie Bressler broke down how deals between brands and influencers come together. Step one usually starts with the brand.

“Whenever we have a launch or a business priority, we’ll talk through where do we need support, who do we want support from and what does that really look like. Then, as the influencer lead, we’ll talk through or present ideas, concepts, creator names that I think would be such a perfect fit for the product. And I will then reach out to someone like Sophie, who I’ve had a great relationship with for over 10 years now,” said Musinsky, director of influence and advocacy at YSL Beauty.

Then the ball is in the creator’s court, or at least the creator’s talent manager, such as Bressler. Brief in hand, she looks for the brand’s objective to identify the creators best suited to the campaign.

“If Hadley says to me, ‘We’re really looking to drive sales’, I don’t want to put forward a client who doesn’t have a history of strong link clicks on Instagram Stories,” Bressler said.

From there, the two sides go back and forth to hash out details, including exclusivity, usage rights and eventually payment terms.

“The payment terms don’t get ironed out until we get to the contracting stage,” said Bressler, who delved into details of those discussions in the episode, along with Musinsky who shared YSL Beauty and parent company’s L’Oréal’s payment terms.

Here are a few highlights from the conversation, which have been edited for length and clarity.

Musinsky: “We usually don’t include links, so we’re not tracking to that extent. Of course, we want to see a spike in sales. But there’s never this goal [of an] exact percentage-increase number. For us, it’s usually more of an awareness play, or as we call it, a virality moment of starting a conversation, having that video or multiple videos perform really well and then seeing other creators feed off of it. Usually that’s our goal.”

Bressler: “I may come to the table and say, ‘I’ve reviewed the scope of work and the creative. Based on that, this is the general starting rate for the [creator] that you’re interested in’. From there it becomes more of a conversation and a friendly negotiation. If what I’ve presented to Hadley is too high for this specific campaign, we would then work backwards to maybe scale back the scope of work, pare back the usage, remove the exclusivity, tweak the deliverables.”

Syndication and usage rights from the brand side

Musinsky: “For the most part, we go in with the offer asking for everything because we know we can start scaling back. If we’re looking for an Instagram post or TikTok, we’ll ask for syndication so that it’s posted across both platforms or even three platforms that will include YouTube Shorts. And then in terms of usage, we’ll usually ask for two weeks paid upfront. We do love to boost content. I think it’s also beneficial for the creator. That’s us putting spend behind it and it running through their account as an ad.”

Exclusivity and usage rights from the creator side

Bressler: “When it comes to paid usage, what is the perceived exclusivity there? Even if there is not technical exclusivity and my client could work with [a separate] luxe beauty brand the next day, if YSL Beauty is running paid ads with my client in it, another luxe beauty brand may not want to collaborate with [the creator] because they think they must be off the table because they’re working with YSL Beauty. We as reps need to understand what other opportunities are on the table at that specific time and weigh the opportunity cost against the desired scope of work in full.”

#7297. Meow Mix reimagines iconic cat memes for multichannel campaign

The J.M. Smucker Co. cat food brand fictionalized behind-the-scenes videos for internet-famous characters including Keyboard Cat and Huh Cat.

Meow Mix’s “Behind the Memeow” campaign piggybacks on popular internet cat memes that continue to be a sizeable part of internet culture — if not quite the 15% of the internet cited in press materials, a persistent figure that originated in a campaign by competitor Friskies over a decade ago.

“Cats have always had a special place in internet culture, and some of the most iconic memes of all time have come from their wonderfully unpredictable personalities,” Nicole Massey, vice president of marketing for pet products at J.M. Smucker Co, said in a statement. “With ‘Behind the Memeow,’ we’re celebrating that cultural legacy while reminding cat parents that even the pickiest, most notoriously hard-to-please cats can’t resist Meow Mix.”

Three 30-second ads feature meme characters Table Cat, Keyboard Cat and Huh Cat and show how Meow Mix changed their behavior. Each spot features new lyrics for the iconic Meow Mix jingle, which the brand has continued to iterate on to reach new generations of consumers.

Beyond the ad spots, the campaign will be amplified with a social-first media strategy that includes paid support across Meta, Pinterest, Snapchat and Reddit, with a high-impact TikTok placement. The effort also includes influencer collaborations and a custom content partnership with youth-focused publisher Pubity.

J.M. Smucker Co. will announce Q2 fiscal 2027 earnings on Nov. 24. The company in August reported better-than-expected net sales in Q1 of fiscal 2027 and lifted its outlook for the full year.

“We have been very disciplined in terms of where we spend dollars, and we have tools that enable us to evaluate how much bang for the buck we get and where we're going to get incremental ROI,” CEO Mark Smucker said of the company’s approach to marketing on an earnings call. “I feel pretty confident that we can be choiceful and prudent with the dollars and put them where we're actually going to get a meaningful return.”

Marketers in the cat care category have used various tactics to engage with pet parents. Clorox’s Fresh Step teamed with artificial intelligence-powered agency RECE to create a series of ads, while Whisker made a marketing push around its Litter-Robot brand.

#7293. Dentsu makes entertainment and sport a C-suite priority amid earnings stumble

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Expertise in entertainment could be the next agency C-suite superpower. Takeshi Sano, the president and global CEO of Japanese agency holding company Dentsu, clearly thinks so: he’s elevated Mitsuyuki ‘Mike’ Nakamura, a former media buyer, TV producer and a 36-year Dentsu veteran, to serve as the group’s first global practice president for sports and entertainment.

Nakamura’s appointment is further proof of the growing importance of entertainment expertise to major ad agencies,  as media spend concentrates around live sports and TV, film and showbiz reknit around the creator economy.

“Sports and entertainment is where culture is made today, and Dentsu has a long track-record of helping brands go further than just buying space around it,” said Sano in a statement.

Nakamura has worked for Dentsu in both Thailand, as CEO of Dentsu Media, and the U.K., where he led its CX business, Dentsu X. Most recently he served as president of sports and entertainment at Dentsu Japan.

Dentsu is more closely coiled with the entertainment sector than most advertising groups. It’s an investor in the upcoming Angry Birds film, which will also feature two of its clients. It has produced microdramas for P&G and recently inked a partnership with Iconic Arts to help brands create their own anime. It’s not alone in moving closer to the showbiz world, though; last week, Ogilvy launched a partnership with top talent agency CAA to use AI clones of its high profile stars in brand advertising.

The company made the announcement the same day that it released its third-quarter earnings report. Overall organic revenue fell 3.7% and the company altered its revenue guidance for the whole of 2026 from 0.1% growth to -1%.

Organic growth reversed in Japan, the Americas (down 6.5%) and Europe, the Middle East and Africa (EMEA), where income fell 7.1% due to “macro uncertainty and reduced business from major clients” hitting media revenues. Dentsu’s media revenues in the Americas have fallen 2.5% this year so far.

The company’s results come amid signs of a potential slowdown in overall global advertising spending. Amazon, Google and Reddit each recorded deceleration in advertising revenues this past quarter; the former saw Amazon ad revenue growth fall from 26% in Q2, to 12% in Q3.

But Dentsu has fared worse than its major agency group rivals. WPP’s revenue for the same period was up 0.5%, while Publicis’ rose 5%.

And Brian Wieser, founder of Madison & Wall, suggested its revenue declines reflect its own circumstances, rather than a broader slowdown – namely the absence of Microsoft from its balance sheet. The company lost the tech giant’s $2.67 billion global account to Publicis back in April.

“Its scale should not be underestimated,” Wieser said. “It was probably by far the biggest client that Dentsu had.”

Spending on ad agencies is increasingly divorced from spending on advertising as a whole, Wieser added. “Marketers have been so focused on containing their non-working spending, as they characterize it, that the growth of spend on [advertising] is independent of the growth of spend on agencies,” he said.

The bottom line is that Dentsu can’t rely on the ad spend tides to raise its boat, but must find its own way back to growth.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

In Q3 earnings, The Trade Desk’s leadership reveals it is searching for answers.

#7291. Deloitte Digital’s new agentic tools aim to fix messy marketing workflows

A new Converge by Front Office suite is designed to help brands better connect their existing technology stacks and wring greater value from them.

Marketers continue to wrestle with the gap between the promise of AI and the reality of the technology’s results, which for some haven’t lived up to the hype. Agencies and other marketing services providers are responding to persistent frustrations around AI fragmentation and execution with a wave of solutions themed around orchestration, or improving workflows.

Seventy-six percent of surveyed brand leaders believe their operating foundations do not meaningfully support realizing value from AI, which CMOs are under growing pressure to master, according to a recent Deloitte Digital report on personalization trends. Nearly all, 97%, of respondents say that the pace of AI development outstrips their ability to optimize AI for personalization purposes. Platform and workflow inefficiencies were also cited as hindrances.

The consultancy’s Converge Front Office suite seeks to address these shortcomings by bringing greater unity to marketers’ AI tech stacks. Agents will work to link existing tools and data, including from cloud-computing and software providers. Clients can also enlist Deloitte engineers to ensure Converge solutions cater to specific needs or adapt as their organization’s priorities evolve.

The Converge Marketing product is designed to break down silos and better integrate campaign strategy, planning, execution and measurement while ensuring marketers retain control over key decisions. Agent capabilities span research, strategy planning, asset building, campaign optimization and performance tracking. The goal is to get marketers from the pilot phase to production faster, Mark Singer, marketing service lead and CMO at Deloitte Digital, said in a statement.

Converge Sales boasts agents that can help identify prospects, engage inbound buyers and manage sales pipeline activity, among other functions. Deloitte Digital is aligning the new Converge brands with Converge Signals, a predictive intelligence platform that draws on data from partners including customer insights firm HundredX and Deloitte Digital’s own BrandWorth scoring system.

Other agencies are racing to hone their competitive edge on the agentic front. Publicis Groupe recently completed its acquisition of data-collaboration platform LiveRamp, a deal the ad-holding group says will enable it to produce more sophisticated agents on behalf of clients.

Deloitte has ramped up its agentic services as some of its consulting divisions experience a slowdown. The company’s technology and transformation unit grew 2.5% in the fiscal year 2026, a lower rate of growth than the prior year.

#7289. Ad Tech Briefing: Google’s antitrust troubles aren’t over

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

Developments last week showed that Google faces another potential antitrust setback, this time in Europe, as it prepares for the next stage of its U.S. ad tech monopoly case.

On Nov. 12, an adviser to the European Union’s highest court recommended overturning a 2024 judgment that annulled an earlier €1.49 billion ($1.7 billion) fine against Google over its AdSense for Search business.

In her ⁠opinion, Advocate General Juliane Kokott argued that the EU’s General Court made legal errors when it overturned the European Commission’s original 2019 penalty. Specifically, Kokott concluded that the lower court had imposed excessive requirements on regulators when assessing whether Google’s contractual restrictions harmed competition.

The case concerns agreements Google used with publishers that displayed search advertisements through its AdSense for Search service; i.e., it involves a different business unit than the one under scrutiny in its most recent U.S. trial.

Per the European proceedings’ plaintiffs, between 2006 and 2016, Google imposed contractual restrictions that limited those websites’ ability to display advertisements from rival search advertising providers.

The European Commission identified three types of restrictions: exclusivity clauses preventing publishers from using competing services, placement clauses restricting where rival advertisements could appear, and provisions requiring Google’s prior approval before changes to advertising displays.

The Commission concluded that these practices constituted a single, continuous infringement of European competition law. Google challenged the decision, and in September 2024, the General Court annulled the fine, finding that regulators had failed to examine relevant circumstances adequately, particularly the duration of Google’s contractual arrangements.

Kokott’s opinion, published Nov. 12, argued that the General Court had placed excessive emphasis on the duration of individual agreements rather than considering their collective effect on competition.

In the accompanying press release, the court explained that regulators could assess contracts collectively to determine whether they could produce exclusionary effects. Kokott recommended setting aside the 2024 judgment and returning the case to the General Court for reconsideration.

Importantly, the recommendation does not automatically reinstate the original fine. The opinion is nonbinding, and the Court of Justice has yet to issue its judgment. Moreover, Kokott acknowledged that the penalty should ultimately be reduced because certain findings concerning Google’s conduct in 2016 are no longer being contested.

Nevertheless, the opinion creates the prospect of another prolonged legal battle over Google’s advertising practices, as the development comes two months after Google avoided a forced breakup of its advertising technology business in the U.S.

In September, federal judge Leonie Brinkema rejected the Justice Department’s proposed divestiture of Google’s AdX exchange and publisher ad server, DoubleClick for Publishers.

Instead, the court accepted most of the government’s proposed behavioral remedies, subject to modifications. The decision shifted the focus from Google’s ownership of its advertising infrastructure to the restrictions governing how that infrastructure operates.

The remedies are intended to address Google’s ability to favor its own advertising products, including through changes affecting interoperability, auction transparency and access for competing ad tech companies. However, the precise obligations and their enforcement remain central questions as the parties work through the final judgment.

Google has indicated that it intends to appeal the underlying liability decision, potentially extending the litigation beyond implementation of the behavioral remedies.

The European and American cases concern different markets and legal questions, but both illustrate the difficulty regulators face in challenging Google’s advertising dominance.

In Europe, the courts are still examining conduct that ended a decade ago. In the U.S., the argument has moved from whether Google violated competition law to whether the remedies will materially change market behavior.

However, even successful antitrust enforcement does not guarantee that publishers and advertisers will move their business away from Google.

Meanwhile, last week’s proceedings demonstrated how The Trade Desk’s struggles highlight a different problem — a development that embodies the scale of the challenges independent competitors to Google, etc., face when challenging Big Tech.

On Nov. 9, The Trade Desk reported a rare quarterly revenue decline, with third-quarter sales falling approximately 0.5% to $736 million from $739 million a year earlier. While the company did beat Wall Street’s (modest) expectations, the result marked a sharp reversal for a company that had long delivered double-digit growth.

The results underscored the difficulties facing the company during what executives have described as a transitional year. The slowdown comes amid growing competition from Amazon, shifting agency relationships and the increasing use of AI to automate media buying.

While The Trade Desk has invested heavily in its Kokai platform and related AI capabilities, those initiatives have yet to restore its historical growth rates, with executives there also revealing how they are reconsidering how acquisitions might contribute to its next phase of growth.

On the company’s earnings call, Jeff Green, The Trade Desk, CEO, revealed leadership were taking a closer look at potential acquisitions, suggesting a more active approach to M&A than the company has historically pursued.

For years, The Trade Desk positioned itself as an independent alternative to the advertising ecosystems operated by Google, Amazon, and other major technology companies, with this pitch a key selling point when articulating its value in the responsible use of CMOs’ media investments.

However, its latest results demonstrate that independence alone is not enough to guarantee growth.

Regulators may eventually make Google’s advertising infrastructure more accessible to competitors. But those competitors must still persuade advertisers that their platforms offer better technology, performance, and economics. The Trade Desk’s difficulties suggest that the industry’s competitive challenges extend well beyond Google’s market power.

#7287. Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

The Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit is the technical owner and architect of the systems that power our go-to-market engine. You'll decide how our GTM stack is designed, what we build versus buy, and where we invest next — and you'll be hands-on building it: Salesforce architecture, integrations, enrichment pipelines, and the agentic workflows that help Sales and Marketing move faster and smarter.

Salesforce is our system of record for revenue, pipeline, and customer data. HubSpot is our marketing layer for campaigns and lead generation, and what it captures needs to flow cleanly and reliably into Salesforce. Around them sits a growing stack of enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and data platform tools. You'll own the architecture that ties it all together: disciplined change management, observable pipelines, data models built for AI consumption, and agentic workflows that act on GTM signals at scale — designed to serve the business for years, not quarters.

This role sits at the intersection of GTM, Data, Finance, and Engineering. You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

Lead build-vs-buy and procurement decisions: evaluate vendors, run proofs of concept, assess security and integration fit, and partner with Director of GTM Systems and Analytics, Finance, Legal, and IT on selection and renewals.

Shape the GTM systems roadmap with senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, RevOps, Finance, and Engineering: align on requirements, sequence investments, make clear tradeoffs, and lead cross-functional initiatives to completion.

Own Salesforce as the engineering system of record for revenue: data model, flows and automation, validation rules, field governance, and access control.

Own HubSpot for campaigns and lead generation, along with the HubSpot-Salesforce sync — so leads, contacts, and engagement signals reach the revenue pipeline without drift or silent failures.

Build and maintain integrations across the GTM stack, and partner with GTM Analytics Engineering on reliable data flows to the central data platform.

Design and deploy agentic workflows that consume GTM data, surface actionable signals, and automate manual operations — and set the patterns and guardrails others build on.

Establish observability and change management for critical GTM systems: alerts, dashboards, and runbooks; reviewed code changes, documented config, and rollback paths.

Mentor engineers across experience levels and set the technical standards for the team.

Partner with the Billing Systems Engineer to automate the CPQ-to-Billing handoff.

Extensive engineering experience in GTM or business systems, including owning a GTM stack's architecture end to end — build-vs-buy calls, vendor evaluations, and systems designed for a multi-year horizon.

Deep, hands-on Salesforce expertise — you understand the data model, have built complex flows and automation, and you think about it as an engineering system, not an admin tool. This is the primary bar.

HubSpot experience in a marketing context, including the HubSpot-Salesforce sync and what breaks and why.

Technical depth beyond the UI — you've built integrations with APIs, webhooks, and event streams, and you know when a native tool (a Flow, a Clay table, a HubSpot workflow) is the right answer versus when it's time to write code.

Agentic workflow experience — you've designed and deployed AI agents that act on GTM data in production.

Strong change management practices — you've shipped changes to live systems while protecting active deals and campaigns.

Experience influencing senior stakeholders — translating business strategy into system requirements and roadmaps.

A history of mentoring engineers, from early-career to senior.

Salesforce CPQ: approvals, order forms, discounting rules, and the closed-won-to-billing path.

SQL and data modeling fluency; familiarity with a warehouse-centric stack (BigQuery, Snowflake, dbt, reverse ETL).

Experience with billing platforms (Stripe, Zuora, Maxio) and the handoffs between GTM systems and billing.

Experience managing a GTM tooling budget or vendor portfolio.

Experience as a technical lead on a GTM or business systems team.

#7286. Holiday marketing hits inflection point in 2026: What brands need to know

Consumers’ push-and-pull relationship with buzzy tactics like AI and tried-and-true strategies like creator partnerships is challenging brands to find the right balance.

The 2026 holiday marketing landscape sits at an inflection point as advertisers address the push-and-pull relationship consumers have with buzzy tactics like artificial intelligence and tried-and-true strategies like creator partnerships and deals-driven messaging. For marketers to win the season, balance will be key.

Despite evolving shopping behaviors, consumers remain bullish for the holidays. Average household spend for the season is expected to reach $1,654, a 4% year-over-year increase, according to Deloitte’s annual holiday survey. Affordability remains a chief concern, but traditional holiday values like human connection and togetherness are what will move the needle for brands this season, according to Deb Freeman, head of strategy at Gut New York.

“The holidays in general are being more cherished by people,” Freeman said. “Marketers are really trying to understand how to take their brand DNA and plug it into the engine of this holiday spirit in a way that is going to come out on the other side full of humanity.”

For many brands, this year’s holiday season is particularly high stakes as they contend with year-round sluggish sales. Playbooks have included a focus on handmade ad creative and creator tie-ups, along with strategic ways to help consumers comb through an oversaturation of deals. Brands are also prioritizing younger audiences including Gen Z and millennials, cohorts that for the first time will make up the bulk of holiday sales, per Deloitte.

One of the chief questions to be answered this season is how much — and in what capacity — consumers are interested in AI within the shopping experience. Over one-third of consumers report being very or extremely willing to use generative AI tools to help with holiday shopping, according to Gartner research shared with Marketing Dive. It’s a different story when it comes to AI-powered marketing materials, which consumers have shown resistance to on a broader scale, said Gartner Senior Principal Analyst Emily Weiss.

“If everyone can click and generate a campaign in Copilot and it only takes 20 minutes — or less — then why would they expect that people should spend any time caring about that?” Weiss said.

A variety of brands are placing a greater emphasis on human-made creative. Earlier this month, Levi’s debuted a campaign, “Let’s Get Fuzzy,” featuring stop-motion claymation. The spot, which required four hours of filming for each second of animation, calls back to the 1995 ad “Clayman,” the brand’s first global commercial. The strategy allowed the brand to celebrate human creativity while flexing its legacy status, a move that could help grow loyalty.

“We often reference and pay homage to our past while moving ideas and creativity forward — and it was this same thinking that we applied to our ‘Let’s Get Fuzzy’ campaign,” said Anne Cambria, vice president of global marketing at Levi Strauss & Co, in emailed comments.

Similarly, E.l.f. Brands released “Made to Stand Out,” a campaign that features a spot made entirely with stop-motion animation. Narrated by actor Alan Cumming, the ad tells the story of a trio of friends as they grow to love their unique qualities. The move, which aligns with the beauty purveyor's longtime focus on individuality, represents its first holiday campaign.

“When we looked at the holiday landscape, we saw a sea of sameness and a real opportunity to bring something different to the table,” said CMO Oshiya Savur, in emailed comments.

While consumers have largely rejected AI in marketing, they have expressed interest in the tech for assistance with more mundane tasks like product discovery and price comparisons. Additionally, 13% of shoppers are using AI for gifting inspiration, according to Gartner.

Consumers who use AI for inspiration consult an average of 4.48 additional sources compared to 3.24 among nonusers. The insight suggests that AI isn’t streamlining the shopping journey, but adding another layer to an already fragmented process, Weiss explained. Retailer websites remain the main source for gifting inspiration, while in-store shopping and Amazon are the top planned purchase channels at 77% and 75%, respectively.

“These tools are not replacing the more traditional search channels or the traditional places where people tend to start their shopping journeys, like good old Google Search, Amazon, friends and family wish lists and other people’s real life recommendations — it’s just that we can’t mine real-life recommendations for data,” Weiss said.

The executive recommends that marketers focus on improving discoverability across both traditional and emerging channels. That includes ensuring that brand-based and retail-based data are communicating with each other, as well as keeping product availability and shipping options up to date, moves that directly affect search rankings.

Consumers’ use of AI for price comparisons comes as affordability remains top of mind, with 79% of shoppers expecting higher prices this season, per Deloitte. The tech may also be a way for shoppers to relieve deal fatigue, as brands have recently flooded shoppers with promotions — the legitimacy of which have come under scrutiny by consumers — in a bid to the budget-conscious.

JCPenney is seeking to combat deal overwhelm with a focus on real shoppers and no-fuss ways to save. A “Party Fits” spot, set in Crown Point, Indiana, stars a local barber who showcases three holiday looks she scored for a total of $168. The campaign is based on the insight that only 32% of shoppers completely or mostly trust retailers to offer a fair price.

Of shoppers this season, 38% are classified by Deloitte as value seekers, or individuals who engage in five or more cost-saving behaviors. While that could raise alarms for brands, value seeking consumers are also expected to spend 12% more than others, making them a key cohort to win over, said Brian McCarthy, a principal in Deloitte’s strategy and analytics practice.

“I think of value seeking as another way of saying engaged shoppers,” McCarthy said.

Some brands have sought to communicate their value by simplifying the process of finding the perfect gift. Target this month debuted a holiday campaign centered on an Academy of Giftology concept where various courses cater to different gift-giving needs. Sephora tapped Seth Rogen for ads that promote in-store Sephora advisors and cater to those who are searching for gifts but are clueless about beauty products.

“The idea was that if Seth can Sephora, we all can Sephora,” said Freeman. Gut New York is the agency behind the “We All Sephora” campaign.

Other marketers are taking a more traditional approach, including Walmart, which debuted ads inspired by “The Muppet Christmas Carol.” Meanwhile, Gap is iterating on its “Give Your Gift” campaign with a community-focused spot. To strike the right mix of emotional and deals-focused advertising, marketers should consider how the two strategies can complement each other to keep consumers engaged throughout the entire holiday season, Freeman explained.

“If you are only thinking about giving people the discounts, the rewards, and banging them over the head with that, they’re going to stop engaging,” Freeman said. “It’s more about the pacing of it [and] thinking about where your audience is at different points of the season.”

This year’s holiday season could be of greater importance for marketers focused on Gen Z and millennials. Together these two cohorts are expected to make up 50% of spending for the first time in the 41 years Deloitte has been running its holiday survey. Key to reaching these audiences will be focusing on preferences, like wish lists, personalized gifts and faster delivery, which matter to them at a higher rate than they do to older generations, McCarthy said.

“Younger shoppers want a bit more guidance, personalization and convenience,” he said. “Gen Xers and boomers in our study want clear value and to shop independently if the clear value is stated.”

Rising creator investments across the industry — one way marketers are attempting to reach digitally native younger consumers — are expected to persist throughout the holiday period. This season will also see an evolution of creator activations as the channel becomes more sophisticated, explained Becky Owen, CMO of Billion Dollar Boy Group.

“I think the way in which brands are integrating into the culture of creators and the culture of social is a lot more interesting,” Owen said. “It feels a lot more driven by psychology, whereas I’d say in years past, it was a lot more about just review it, get it out there, build hype, build noise, and the loudest brand wins.”

Strategic creator activations will be key as consumers show signs of influencer fatigue while brands sink billions into the channel. One way that sentiment has manifested this season is through strategies that go beyond lavish product hauls — a move many see as out of touch — to include more intentional plays like advent calendars, which can encourage earned content and product discovery if done correctly, Owen explained. Skims recently came under fire for its take on the trend, which included a $148 advent calendar filled with hair ties and bobby pins.

Brands could also build stronger consumer trust by prioritizing long-term partnerships and ambassador programs, which have recently grown in popularity, Owen said. Such is the focus for Urban Outfitters: The brand recently expanded on its “All Together Now” brand platform with “All Together Now: More the Merrier,” a campaign inspired by the insight that the majority of Gen Z plans to self-gift this season and values togetherness.

For the launch, Urban Outfitters hosted a party that included product customization stations and an immersive gifting suite from partner DoorDash. The move also brought together people who featured in other marketing pushes from the brand earlier this year, including student ambassadors and attendees of an experience in Joshua Tree, California.

“As we seek out partnerships, we’re not just looking for short-term, we’re really looking for community members that have an affinity for the brand on their own, because that’s where we get the magic,” said Julia Draper, head of digital and e-commerce at Urban Outfitters.

While the holiday season is just kicking into high gear, one takeaway is clear: Brands are willing to pull out all the stops to come out ahead during the end-of-year period. As they plan around evolving consumer behavior and map out ways to leverage flashy tactics, Gut’s Freeman emphasizes that at the center of every strategy should be a focus on the values that define the season.

“People are looking for connection,” Freeman said in an emailed statement. “They want stories they can relate to, laugh about and feel sentimental toward. They want to feel like they're part of something.”

The sense of a stable middle ground is eroding across an industry at a point of contraction, with many displaying mixed feelings about marketing’s future.

As promises of one-stop, AI-powered solutions proliferate, marketers will need to navigate the changed ecosystem and separate reality from hype.

#7284. What exactly counts as a creator? The industry still can’t agree

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Every brand seems to have different definitions when it comes to the creator economy. What one brand calls a tastemaker, another labels an influencer. If marketers can’t agree on creator taxonomy, standardization — on everything from measurement to cost — may remain further away than the industry would like.

Since the ballooning of the creator economy, marketers have been trying to standardize everything from what counts as a view, brand-creator contractual language and the framework for deal fee structures. According to the IAB’s latest creator economy report, at least 23% of marketers say the industry would benefit from a unified creator taxonomy.

“That’s the blurry line. Who’s a mega influencer and who’s a celebrity? I don’t know anymore,” said Mindy Murray, director of influencer and content at General Mills.

General Mills’ approach to creator definitions is nuanced. The highest tier is celebrity status, where partnerships often go beyond social and into product design and formula. Mega creators are positioned just below celebrity partnerships. Mid creators range from 500,000 to 1.5 million social media followers. There are super fans or brand advocates who are dedicated creators who are positing consistently on behalf of the brand. Finally, there’s friends and family, which is General Mills’ organic influencer group who also act as brand advocates.

Shutterfly has a three-tier creator codification. Tastemakers are social media stars with one million or more followers. They’re handpicked for major campaigns. Content creators fall on the other end of the spectrum, having anywhere from a couple hundred to more than 2,000 social media followers. Influencers fall somewhere in between, Callie Worthington, vp of brand marketing and creative at Shutterfly, told Digiday.

Shutterfly doesn’t have an internal pay scale that relates to its codification system. It is expected, however, that bigger creators (which could be tastemakers and influencers) command bigger budgets.

Rothy’s shoe brand also uses the term tastemakers. However, Rothy’s creator classifications are defined less by follower count and more by their cultural relevance, said Anna Doré, vp of brand and communications at Rothy’s. A tastemaker could be a TikTok star or a Substack writer. In comparison, Rothy’s refers to affiliate creators as “the lower funnel crater and conversion types,” Doré said.

Meanwhile, agencies don’t seem to go by the same guidelines. For example, VML organizes creators by their role in a campaign — salespeople, brand ambassadors, product collaborators, etc. The taxonomy isn’t as strict, Noah Eisemann, global executive director of creator and influencer at VML, told Digiday.

“Every influencer is a creator, but not every creator is an influencer,” Eisemann said.

The creator economy has exploded to where creators aren’t just selling products on behalf of brands. They’ve been hired as actors in Super Bowl spots. They’ve been brought on as product collaborators. Some are acting as creative agencies helping brands ideate and execute creative campaigns. Kim Kardashian is as much a content creator as she is an influencer, depending on what the brand asks of her, he added.

“The more useful standard may be a shared way of defining the role a creator is playing in a particular strategy — because that’s ultimately what should determine how we value the partnership and what success looks like,” Eisemann said.

A brand’s tiering setup influences how creators are evaluated, how their impact is measured and how they’re utilized in campaigns.

“When expectations, budgets, and measurement rely on different assumptions, effective work can be undervalued, and investment becomes harder to justify or sustain,” said Stefani Stamatiou, CEO of Americas at HYDP creator marketing agency.

But across the industry, there’s no conventional naming system. (Even Digiday staffers couldn’t agree on what defines a creator versus influencer.) And as more money pours into the creator economy, marketers are expecting measurement and business outcomes.

“Greater clarity around inputs and expected outcomes would make it easier to set goals, allocate budgets, and define the role creators play within the broader advertising landscape,” Stamatiou said.

A new product manager role will explore ad-related monetization, suggesting the platform could make ads a bigger part of its business.

CEOs have never cared much for holdcos. Enterprise solutions, on the other hand, get a meeting.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

#7283. Burger King’s feedback campaign takes aim at its own restaurants

CMO Joel Yashinsky explains how the chain is focused on listening to customers, a push that has helped the chain deliver its sixth consecutive sales increase.

Burger King is asking consumers to help the brand determine which of its restaurants need a remodel or renovation, per details shared with Marketing Dive. “Rebuild My BK” continues the recent efforts to turn feedback into action as part of the brand’s larger turnaround plan.

“One of those key pieces [of feedback] that we heard — and we certainly had an inkling to this, as well — was our restaurants needed to be updated,” said Burger King CMO Joel Yashinsky. “It's a natural evolution of this campaign that takes us beyond just a marketing or promotional event, but is really about the betterment of the brand.”

Along with a microsite for soliciting feedback, the campaign includes a 60-second spot that showcases Burger King President Tom Curtis as he tours the country, meets with consumers and pitches in on construction. The chain has remodeled over 60% of its footprint and is working to open a new or remodeled location every day this year, per the spot.

Curtis has been central to Burger King’s marketing efforts this year, including a push for feedback that saw the chain give out his phone number, a move that has generated 120,000 calls, per press materials. The chain this summer also rolled out a reimagined restaurant manager role and a Whopper Guarantee that yielded free burgers for unsatisfied customers.

“[Tom’s] commitment to listening to the guests, to wanting to hear from them and then being out front in a lot of our creative and our content — he's a natural,” Yashinsky said. “His humility comes across, his authenticity comes across… he didn't want to be the face of the brand, necessarily.”

“Rebuild My BK” is scheduled to run through Jan. 5. Members of Burger King’s Royal Perks loyalty program who complete a submission earn 100 bonus Crowns. Guest feedback is one of several factors the chain may consider when making its remodeling and renovation plans.

Restaurant upgrades and marketing investments have been the major focus of a Reclaim the Flame turnaround plan that Burger King parent Restaurant Brands International launched in 2022. Burger King this year surpassed Wendy’s to reclaim second place in the U.S. burger business behind McDonald’s. The chain saw its sixth-consecutive comparable sales increase, up 8.4% in the U.S. last quarter.

“Our guests are engaging with us. They're on this journey with us, and we're trying to be really true and authentic to them by making these changes,” Yashinsky said. “That's the special piece — the special ingredient, if you will — in what we're doing with this campaign: it's really putting listening to the guest at the forefront.”

#7281. Senior Manager, Business Operations at Clutch

Clutch is at a pivotal moment. We're growing significantly year over year, and we need more connective tissue to keep pace. We're hiring a Senior Manager, Business Operations to connect the commercial side of the house, so the teams that win, onboard, and retain our customers work from the same facts and toward the same goals. This role sits in Finance.

This is a people manager role. You'll lead our Revenue Operations (RevOps) team and act as an operating partner to senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, Customer Success, Implementation, People, and Finance. Together with the RevOps team, you own the shared language of the business (definitions, data, and processes) so commercial teams make decisions from one view of it.

When something important doesn't have an owner yet, it lands with you: a partnership strategy, a new commercial model, or pricing and margin guidance for leadership. This is a fast-moving startup. We're building toward a much larger company but we aren't one yet, and most of this role has no playbook. You'll define the process, build the tool, and hand it to the team. If you want established structure, this likely isn't the role.

You'll report to the Director of Business Operations and work directly with senior leaders across the company as a neutral partner to every function rather than an advocate for one. Your work will be visible, and it will significantly influence how we make commercial decisions.

You'll lead a small, high-ownership RevOps team that runs the systems and data our commercial teams depend on, and creates leverage for them to do their jobs even better. The team doesn't just own systems. They build automation, tooling, and processes, and they're close partners to Sales and Marketing. You'll coach them, set priorities, and open up more strategic scope, while giving them real autonomy.

Learn the business end to end: the revenue funnel, the customer lifecycle, how each commercial team works day to day, and where decisions get stuck

Build trust with your RevOps team: learn the work they own, and set priorities and team rhythms together

Deliver the first version of client-level margin and cost-to-serve, the analysis that grounds our pricing strategy

Develop a first point of view on pricing and packaging for revenue leadership, built on that analysis

Put AI to work in your own workflow from week one, and identify the first set of manual, repetitive processes across the commercial teams worth automating

Stand up a first version of a deal desk, including an approval workflow, pricing and discount guardrails, and standard commercial terms

Partner with Sales and Marketing leaders as they prepare for the monthly business review: pressure-test their plans and point out risks, then solve them with the RevOps team by creating new processes, tooling, or automation

Build on the strength of the RevOps team: prioritize its roadmap against business outcomes and take on more strategic work together

Launch your first AI-enabled workflows or automations that remove manual reporting or reconciliation for the commercial teams, with time saved tracked

Have a team whose output and standards hold, with the processes, tooling, and automation you've built running without you in the room

Have AI-enabled, self-serve tooling that commercial teams use day to day, with measurable time savings

Be the person leadership trusts to pick up the next important thing that doesn't have an owner yet

5-8 years of total experience that combines consulting or banking with time as an operator inside a company

Experience managing people: you've coached ICs, given direct feedback, made tough talent calls, and built team rhythms

Startup experience. You've worked in a fast-moving, ever-changing environment where structure wasn't already there, and you built it. This is not a role for someone whose experience is only at large, established enterprises.

Strong analytical and financial modeling skills, including forecasting. You're very comfortable building models in spreadsheets from scratch, and they hold up to scrutiny. You use AI to move faster and sharpen your work, and you have the foundational modeling knowledge to know when its output is wrong.

Data-first and self-sufficient. You open the dataset before you build the slide, you're always looking for more data to test an idea, and you're comfortable getting into raw data yourself. You use AI to analyze it faster and know enough to check its work.

Commercial acumen: you can read commercial terms, spot the problematic ones, and reason about pricing, discounting, margin, and deal structure

A clear communicator who can walk an executive through a messy problem and leave them with a decision, and who is just as effective working alongside an AE, a CSM, or an engineer

Comfort with ambiguity. When something isn't set up yet, your instinct is to build it, not to wait for it. You create structure, manage competing priorities, and pivot when things change.

Relentless follow-through: you close loops, and people stop checking on what you've said you'll do

A partner's temperament: you challenge constructively without taking sides, you surface problems early, and you resolve cross-team conflict without escalating it

Industry experience in fintech, financial services, lending, banking, or credit unions

#7280. KERV.ai, Macmillan Publishers and Chomps are among this year’s Work/Life Awards finalists

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This year’s Work/Life Awards finalists reflect a landscape where companies are rethinking workplace culture as a driver of business performance, employee engagement and innovation. Across industries, organizations are giving employees a more active role in shaping workplace policies and priorities, building recognition and connection into the everyday employee experience and extending their missions through community involvement and social impact.

KERV.ai earned a finalist spot in the Most Innovative Culture category for making employee input and cross-functional collaboration central to its workplace culture. Its quarterly HR listening tours, culture committee and real-time feedback programs gave employees opportunities to influence company policies and engagement initiatives. The emphasis on employee voice also extended to the business. Employees across product, engineering, client services and creative identified an opportunity to improve contextual targeting, leading to the development of Moment Match Engine, a video technology solution built through cross-departmental collaboration.

Macmillan Publishers is nominated in the Most Committed to Social Good category for connecting its workplace culture to a broader mission of expanding access to books, literacy and intellectual freedom. Through its MacImpact corporate social responsibility program, the publisher supports employee volunteering, charitable giving and community partnerships. In 2025, employees took part in 27 volunteer events and contributed 494 volunteer hours, a 60% increase from the previous year. Its employee-led Yours to Read Committee also advocates for access to diverse literature and the freedom to read.

Chomps, a finalist for Most Committed to Employee Appreciation, focused on maintaining employee connection as its remote-first workforce grew to more than 200 people across 30-plus states. The company brings employees together through annual retreats, departmental offsites and company-wide gatherings, while recognition programs such as Chompian of the Quarter and Chompian of the Year celebrate employee contributions. Investments in wellness, mental health days and professional development further extend appreciation beyond formal recognition.

Together, this year’s nominees represent a workplace landscape in which culture is shaped by employee participation, meaningful recognition and a commitment to a broader purpose beyond business goals. As expectations of employers continue to evolve, their approaches offer examples of how organizations can build more connected, engaged and purpose-driven workplaces.

#7278. Some creators are turning YouTube back catalogs into sizable paychecks

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Creators are turning their back catalogs into fresh revenue as platforms like Tubi hunt for more content to meet audience demand.

One true-crime creator, who requested anonymity, earned $154,000 across Spotify, Tubi, and Facebook this past September — more than 1.8 times what their channel made in the YouTube Partner Program that month, according to Chanel Simmons, md of digital media partner Motion Society, who did not provide specific YouTube figures.

That kind of return is part of a broader push to make existing content work harder. Motion Society has been syndicating creator content since 2022, but more creators and platforms are approaching them to help syndicate content now than ever. It works with over 650 creators and IP owners to distribute more than 30,000 videos a month across over 20 different digital platforms and TVs.

For Reign Maker Group CEO and co-founder Jonathan Chanti, the opportunity reflects a shift in creator strategy. “The focus is shifting from audience growth to maximizing the value of the content creators already own. Creators have spent the last decade learning how to produce content at scale. The next challenge is learning how to distribute and monetize it at scale,” he said. “Syndication is the natural evolution.”

Syndication is becoming increasingly popular in the creator space and Motion Society isn’t the only company investing in it. Lauren Lyster, vp and head of social media at Go Fish Digital, noted that agencies and managers can help individual creators syndicate. She pointed out that companies like Jellysmack have worked with creators like MrBeast and Brad Mondo, and Mirage Digital’s focus is on helping creators turn their catalogs into episodic content.

“Syndication is kind of like the new MCN [multi-channel network], but rebuilt around distribution instead of YouTube ad sales. The big bonus is that the destinations now pay. So the real value from the syndicators is operational,” said Keith Bendes, chief strategy officer at Linqia.

Kudzi Chikumbu, vp of creator partnerships at Tubi, said its audiences are always looking for authentic creator content. “This is why we look for creators whose body of work has a compelling, distinctive voice and aligns with Tubi’s fandoms, such as horror, YA, and reality,” he said. Tubi licenses content from creators like Joey Graceffa, who makes cinematic murder mystery episodes, and Kinigra Deon, who produces scripted drama and comedy series. Chikumbu said Tubi looks to partner with creators who have built something people are already invested in, which offers monetization in the form of revenue share.

“These platforms need a steady flow of programming, and creator content can help fill that need while bringing a built-in fan base with it,” said Lyster.

For creators, syndication can open additional revenue streams, but adapting and placing a catalog across platforms takes time and operational work. Companies like Motion Society handle that process, typically through revenue-share deals.

It starts its creator syndication process with curation, selecting and sequencing videos from catalogs into cohesive seasons. Then, the company re-edits the content to TV standards, which involves cutting out sponsor reads and social calls to action, removing watermarks, and editing out anything that doesn’t meet TV brand safety guidelines. The company will also cut out YouTube-specific content like mentions of previous videos or calls to follow or burned-in captions. Motion Society designers then make original key art for each show and season in platform-specific formats, and even A/B test strategies across platforms. All of this work saves creators the time and effort of adapting their catalogs for each platform. Motion Society typically takes a revenue share though it declined to disclose details.

These kinds of syndication partnerships give creators another way to monetize videos they’ve already made. As platforms seek more programming, distributors are betting there’s room to place more creator catalogs.

“Free streaming TV services are especially well positioned [to grab creators’ libraries]. Tubi has been openly building programs for creators, and services like Roku, Samsung TV Plus and Pluto TV also carry a lot of digital-first content. These platforms need a steady flow of programming, and creator content can help fill that need while bringing a built-in fan base with it,” said Lyster. She found Spotify an “interesting fit” for creators who do podcast-style video, and thinks Facebook works best for long-form content.

Syndication at this scale could result in shifting terms for brand partnerships. “Who owns the extra reach?” asked Bendes. “If brands are sponsoring the creator’s content and it gets syndicated, does that sponsorship carry? Usage rights, exclusivity windows and brand-safety clauses will need to catch up.”

Bendes expects sponsorship pricing for syndicated creators to move towards TV-style negotiation, with “a premium for multi-surface reach, and a lot of pushback from brands asking for proof that the reach is real.”

Lyster wondered if creators will start to fold syndication into how they run their business and where they post content and potentially follow a model more similar to how musicians use a distributor to get their music onto every streaming service.

Both Chanti and Lyster also noted that creator control could become increasingly more crucial if syndication takes off.

“The biggest risks are creators sacrificing control over their IP, audience relationships or future monetization opportunities for relatively modest short-term gains. Ownership, transparency and favorable licensing terms are critical,” Chanti said.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

Bustle Digital Group has teamed up with Profound to help brands like Pampers and Innisfree boost their visibility in AI recommendations in a new GEO-offering.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

#7276. Holiday TV ads from retailers arrive earlier, more frequently in 2026

While retail historically commands the season, it accounted for 63.7% of holiday ads in October, pacing ahead of recent years.

The holidays are always a busy time for advertising, and Nielsen’s data show activity moving further up the calendar as brands try to make the most of a packed Q4. More campaigns are starting as early as September, with holiday creative this year accounting for 1% of all linear TV ads during the month. Even as ad-supported streaming expands, linear remains on a growth trajectory after surpassing $1 billion in spend in 2023, according to Nielsen.

Some elements of the holidays remain constant, including retail’s dominance, which is particularly pronounced so far this season. The category has commanded an average of 45% of linear TV ad spend over the past five years, and only a slightly smaller share of the burgeoning streaming environment. In October, 63.7% of holiday ads came from retailers.

Promotional messaging is a major theme, with over one-third of retail ads assessed by Nielsen over the past five years carrying mentions of Black Friday or Cyber Monday deals events. Retailers are currently under pressure to reach wary shoppers during a persistent run of inflation. Several campaigns have been themed around more thoughtful, personalized gifting instead of low prices alone.

Effective holiday strategies can boost performance, per Nielsen connected TV benchmark studies for 2025. Cosmetics brands recorded a 3.2 times sales lift compared to non-holiday campaigns while department stores experienced a 2.4 times sales lift, the measurement firm found. Restaurants and fast-food brands recorded sales lifts of 1.5 times and 1 times, respectively, versus non-holiday ads.

Outside of retail, category spending trends have fluctuated more noticeably over time. Automotive shifted from representing 16% of holiday linear TV ad spend in 2023 to just 7.7% in 2025. Nielsen attributed the retreat to a bigger focus on streaming, where auto makes up 18.4% of the market. A dearth of car commercials, once a fixture of prime time programming, has gained media attention as the industry navigates economic headwinds and the failure of electric vehicle bets to take off in the U.S.

Food and beverage advertising also dipped between 2024 and 2025, which Nielsen attributed to big brands tightening their budgets and challenger brands only running smaller experiments for the holidays. Large food and beverage marketers including Pepsi and Oreo have rolled out holiday efforts in the past few weeks, with the latter introducing its first new seasonal creative in five years.
